**`domains/agriculture/seed-saving.md`**
```markdown
# Seed Saving

> **Domain:** agriculture | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** green

## Overview

Saved seed preserves genetic diversity and independence. Open-pollinated
(heirloom) varieties breed true; hybrids do not.

## Prerequisites

- [Soil Testing](soil-testing.md)
- [Crop Rotation](crop-rotation.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Healthy plants (select best specimens)
- Cloth bags
- Glass jars
- Silica desiccant

## Procedure

### Self-Pollinating (Tomato, Bean, Pea, Lettuce)

1. Let fruits mature fully on the plant.
2. For tomatoes: ferment seeds in water 3 days to remove gel.
3. Rinse, dry on a plate (not paper towel).
4. Store in a cool, dark, dry place.

### Cross-Pollinating (Corn, Squash, Brassicas)

1. Isolate varieties by 400 m or use hand-pollination.
2. Bag unopened flowers; hand-pollinate with a brush.
3. Re-bag until fruit sets.

### Biennials (Carrot, Onion, Cabbage)

1. Overwinter roots/bulbs.
2. Replant in spring.
3. Harvest seed heads when dry.

### Storage

1. Dry seeds to <10% moisture (they snap, not bend).
2. Store in glass jars with desiccant.
3. Label: species, variety, year, source.
4. Cool (5–10°C), dark, dry. Viability: 3–10 years.

## Safety Notes

- Do not save seed from diseased plants.
- Do not save seed from F1 hybrids.
- Label meticulously — misidentified seed is worse than no seed.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-03-08

## Sources

- Seed Savers Exchange principles (public domain)
- USDA Seed Saving Guide (public domain)
```

**`sidecars/agriculture/seed-saving.pnk.yaml`**

**`domains/agriculture/crop-rotation.md`**
```markdown
# Crop Rotation

> **Domain:** agriculture | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** green

## Overview

Rotation breaks pest cycles, balances nutrients, and prevents soil depletion.
Rule: never plant the same family in the same bed two years in a row.

## Prerequisites

- [Soil Testing](soil-testing.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Map of plots
- Seeds for 4 families

## Procedure

### The Four-Family Rotation

| Year | Bed 1 | Bed 2 | Bed 3 | Bed 4 |
|------|-------|-------|-------|-------|
| 1 | Legume | Brassica | Root | Fruiting |
| 2 | Brassica | Root | Fruiting | Legume |
| 3 | Root | Fruiting | Legume | Brassica |
| 4 | Fruiting | Legume | Brassica | Root |

Families:
- **Legume:** pea, bean, clover — fixes nitrogen
- **Brassica:** cabbage, broccoli, kale — heavy feeder
- **Root:** carrot, beet, onion — loosens soil
- **Fruiting:** tomato, pepper, squash — heavy feeder

### Cover Crops

- Winter: rye, vetch, clover
- Summer: buckwheat, cowpea
- Chop and till in before seed set to add organic matter.

### Fallow

- Rest a bed every 4 years.
- Plant with a legume cover and let it mature.

## Safety Notes

- Do not rotate tomatoes with potatoes (same family, shared diseases).
- Do not follow a legume with another legume.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-03-10

## Sources

- USDA Crop Rotation Guide (public domain)
- FAO Sustainable Agriculture (public domain)
```

**`domains/agriculture/grain-cultivation.md`**
```markdown
# Grain Cultivation

> **Domain:** agriculture | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** green

## Overview

Grains (wheat, rice, maize, barley, millet) provide the calorie base of
civilization. They store dry for years.

## Prerequisites

- [Soil Testing](soil-testing.md)
- [Crop Rotation](crop-rotation.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Seed grain
- Plow or hoe
- Sickle or scythe
- Threshing surface
- Winnowing basket
- Mill (see [Mills](../materials/mills.md))

## Procedure

### Soil Preparation

1. Test soil; amend with compost to pH 6–7.
2. Plow to 15–20 cm.
3. Harrow to break clods.

### Sowing

1. Broadcast or drill seed at the rate for your grain:
   - Wheat: 100–150 kg/ha
   - Rice (transplanted): 40–60 kg/ha
   - Maize: 20–25 kg/ha
2. Cover lightly with soil.

### Growing

1. Water: 400–650 mm per season for wheat; rice needs flooding.
2. Weed at 3 weeks and 6 weeks.
3. Watch for rust, blight, and locusts.

### Harvest

1. Cut when grain is hard and heads nod.
2. Bundle into shocks; dry in the field 1–2 weeks.
3. Thresh by beating bundles on a hard surface.
4. Winnow by tossing in the wind.

### Storage

1. Dry grain to <12% moisture (bites crack, not dent).
2. Store in airtight containers with desiccant.
3. Add diatomaceous earth to prevent weevils.
4. Cool, dark, dry place. Viability: 5–20 years.

## Safety Notes

- Ergot (fungal infection of rye) is toxic — discard black grains.
- Grain dust is explosive; ventilate threshing areas.
- Rice paddies breed mosquitoes; manage standing water.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-03-12

## Sources

- FAO Cereal Production Guide (public domain)
- USDA Small Grains Manual (public domain)
```

**`domains/agriculture/beekeeping.md`**
```markdown
# Beekeeping

> **Domain:** agriculture | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Bees pollinate ⅓ of crops and produce honey, wax, and propolis. A single
hive can yield 20–40 kg of honey per year.

## Prerequisites

- [Simple Tools](../survival/simple-tools.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Hive (Langstroth or top-bar)
- Smoker
- Hive tool
- Bee suit, gloves, veil
- Sugar syrup (1:1 for spring, 2:1 for fall)

## Procedure

### Catching a Swarm

1. Find a swarm on a branch in spring.
2. Shake into a box; cover quickly.
3. Transfer to a hive at dusk.

### Building a Colony

1. Install the swarm; leave alone 1 week.
2. Inspect weekly in spring/summer.
3. Feed sugar syrup until comb is drawn.

### Harvesting Honey

1. Wait until ⅔ of cells are capped.
2. Brush bees off frames; use a bee escape board.
3. Uncapp with a knife.
4. Extract with a centrifuge or crush-and-strain.
5. Leave 15 kg honey for the colony's winter.

### Winterizing

1. Reduce entrance.
2. Feed 2:1 syrup in fall.
3. Insulate; ensure ventilation to prevent condensation.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Bee stings can cause anaphylaxis. Have epinephrine if allergic.
- Smoke calms but does not sedate; work slowly.
- Africanized bees are aggressive — do not work them without experience.

## Verification

- **Status:** draft
- **Date:** 2026-03-15

## Sources

- USDA Beekeeping Handbook (public domain)
- FAO Bee Products Guide (public domain)
```

**`domains/agriculture/food-preservation.md`**
```markdown
# Food Preservation

> **Domain:** agriculture | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Preservation extends harvest into winter. Methods: drying, salting, smoking,
canning, fermenting, and root cellaring.

## Prerequisites

- [Making Fire](../survival/making-fire.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Drying rack
- Salt
- Glass jars with lids
- Smokehouse
- Root cellar

## Procedure

### Sun Drying

1. Slice food 3–6 mm thick.
2. Lay on a rack in full sun, covered with mesh.
3. Turn daily; dry 2–5 days until leathery.
4. Rehydrate in water before use.

### Salting

1. Layer food with coarse salt (1 kg salt per 4 kg food).
2. Weight down; keep submerged in brine.
3. Ready in 2–6 weeks.
4. Rinse before eating.

### Smoking

1. Build a small fire in a smokehouse.
2. Use hardwood (hickory, oak, apple). Never resinous softwoods.
3. Cold smoke (20–30°C) for 1–3 days for preservation.
4. Hot smoke (60–80°C) for immediate consumption.

### Water-Bath Canning (High-Acid Foods)

1. Sterilize jars in boiling water.
2. Fill with hot food, leaving 1 cm headspace.
3. Seal; process in boiling water for the recipe time.
4. Cool; check seal (lid should not flex).

### Pressure Canning (Low-Acid Foods)

1. Use a pressure canner at 10 PSI (115°C) for low-acid foods (meat, beans).
2. Water-bath canning does **not** kill botulism spores.

### Fermenting

1. Submerge vegetables in 2% brine.
2. Keep anaerobic; weight down.
3. Ferment 1–4 weeks at 18–22°C.
4. Refrigerate when sour to taste.

### Root Cellaring

1. Ideal: 0–4°C, 90–95% humidity, dark.
2. Store root vegetables in sand or straw.
3. Ventilate to prevent ethylene buildup.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Botulism from improperly canned low-acid foods is often fatal.
- Discard any can with bulging lid, off-smell, or cloudiness.
- Boil all home-canned low-acid food for 10 minutes before eating.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-03-18

## Sources

- USDA Complete Guide to Home Canning (public domain)
- FAO Food Preservation Manual (public domain)
```

**`domains/agriculture/raising-livestock.md`**
```markdown
# Raising Livestock

> **Domain:** agriculture | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Livestock convert grass and waste into protein, fat, leather, and fertilizer.
Start small — chickens and rabbits first, then goats or pigs, then cattle.

## Prerequisites

- [Composting](composting.md)
- [Emergency Shelter](../survival/emergency-shelter.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Fencing
- Feed (grain, hay, forage)
- Water source
- Shelter
- Veterinary basics (see [Health](../health/README.md))

## Procedure

### Chickens

1. Coop: 0.4 m² per bird, roosts 30 cm off ground.
2. Feed: 100 g layer pellets per bird per day + forage.
3. Water: 250 mL per bird per day.
4. Eggs: 1 per hen per day in peak season.
5. Slaughter at 18–24 months for stewing hens.

### Rabbits

1. Hutch: 0.5 m² per adult.
2. Feed: hay ad libitum, 100 g pellets, greens.
3. Breeding: 1 buck per 10 does; gestation 30 days.
4. Yield: 4–8 kg meat per year per doe.

### Goats (Dairy)

1. Shelter: 2 m² per goat, dry bedding.
2. Feed: browse + 1 kg hay + 0.5 kg grain per day.
3. Milk: 2–4 L per day, twice daily.
4. Breeding: seasonal; gestation 150 days.

### Pigs

1. Pen: 10 m² per pig.
2. Feed: kitchen waste, grain, forage.
3. Farrowing: 1 litter per year, 8–12 piglets.
4. Slaughter at 6–8 months (100 kg).

### Cattle

1. Pasture: 0.4–1 ha per cow.
2. Feed: grass/hay + minerals.
3. Milk: 15–30 L/day for dairy breeds.
4. Breeding: 1 bull per 30 cows; gestation 280 days.

### Health Basics

- Quarantine new animals 2 weeks.
- Vaccinate per local disease profile.
- Deworm quarterly.
- Isolate sick animals immediately.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Zoonotic diseases (brucellosis, rabies, salmonella) transmit from animals.
- Always wash hands after handling.
- Bulls and boars can be aggressive; never turn your back.
- Slaughter humanely; stun before bleeding.

## Verification

- **Status:** draft
- **Date:** 2026-03-20

## Sources

- FAO Livestock Production Manual (public domain)
- USDA Small-Scale Livestock Guide (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/charcoal-production.md`**
```markdown
# Charcoal Production

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** novice | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Charcoal burns hotter and cleaner than wood and is essential for smelting
and blacksmithing. It is made by pyrolysis — burning wood with limited oxygen.

## Prerequisites

- [Making Fire](../survival/making-fire.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Hardwood (oak, hickory, mesquite)
- Earth or sand
- Shovel
- Water (for quenching)

## Procedure

### Earth Kiln Method

1. Clear a 2 m circle; level the ground.
2. Stack hardwood in a dome, leaving a central chimney.
3. Cover the pile with leaves, then earth, leaving the chimney open.
4. Light the chimney.
5. When smoke turns from white to blue, close the chimney.
6. Tend for 3–7 days, patching cracks as they appear.
7. When smoke stops, wait 24 hours; then open and quench with water.

### Retort Method (Higher Yield)

1. Place wood in a sealed metal drum.
2. Heat from outside; volatile gases escape via a pipe.
3. Those gases can fuel the fire — self-sustaining once started.
4. Cool before opening.

### Yield

- Earth kiln: 10–20% by weight
- Retort: 25–35%

### Uses

- Smelting iron, copper
- Blacksmithing forges
- Water filtration (activated charcoal)
- Gunpowder (see [Chemistry](chemistry.md))
- Medicinal (charcoal slurry for poisoning)

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Carbon monoxide from smoldering charcoal kills without warning. Work outdoors.
- Never seal a drum while hot — pressure builds.
- Quench thoroughly; buried embers can reignite days later.

## Verification

- **Status:** field-tested
- **Date:** 2026-03-22
- **Location:** Missouri, USA

## Sources

- Traditional charcoal-making methods (public domain)
- FAO Charcoal Production Manual (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/metal-smelting.md`**
```markdown
# Metal Smelting

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Smelting extracts metal from ore using heat and a reducing agent (charcoal).
Start with copper (1,085°C), then bronze (alloy), then iron (1,538°C).

## Prerequisites

- [Charcoal Production](charcoal-production.md)
- [Simple Tools](../survival/simple-tools.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Ore (malachite for copper; hematite/magnetite for iron)
- Charcoal
- Clay furnace or bloomery
- Bellows
- Hammer and anvil

## Procedure

### Finding Ore

- **Copper:** green-stained rocks (malachite, azurite).
- **Iron:** red-brown rocks (hematite), magnetic black sand (magnetite).
- **Lead:** galena (gray cubic crystals).
- **Tin:** cassiterite (heavy black grains in stream beds).

### Building a Furnace

1. Build a clay cylinder 50 cm tall, 30 cm diameter.
2. Add a tuyere (air pipe) at the base for bellows.
3. Line with clay; let dry slowly.

### Copper Smelting

1. Crush ore to 1 cm pieces.
2. Layer charcoal and ore in furnace.
3. Light; pump bellows continuously.
4. After 45 min, tap slag; collect molten copper at base.
5. Yield: 5–20% copper by ore weight.

### Iron Bloomery

1. Same furnace, taller (1 m).
2. Alternate charcoal and crushed iron ore.
3. Pump bellows for 4–6 hours.
4. Extract a "bloom" — spongy iron mixed with slag.
5. Hammer the bloom while hot to expel slag (wrought iron).

### Bronze (Alloy)

1. Melt copper (1,085°C).
2. Add tin (10–12% by weight).
3. Cast into molds. Bronze is harder than copper and easier to cast.

### Cast Iron

1. Higher carbon content (2–4%).
2. Requires higher temp (1,200°C+) and continuous blast.
3. Brittle but hard; cast into shapes.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Molten metal causes severe burns. Wear leather and eye protection.
- Metal fumes (lead, zinc, arsenic) are toxic — ventilate.
- Never pour molten metal onto wet surfaces (steam explosion).
- Carbon monoxide from charcoal — work outdoors.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-03-25

## Sources

- Traditional metallurgy (public domain)
- US Bureau of Mines historical reports (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/blacksmithing.md`**
```markdown
# Blacksmithing

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Blacksmithing shapes wrought iron and steel by heating and hammering. It
produces tools, weapons, hardware, and fasteners.

## Prerequisites

- [Charcoal Production](charcoal-production.md)
- [Metal Smelting](metal-smelting.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Forge (clay or steel)
- Anvil (or dense stone)
- Hammer (2–4 kg)
- Tongs
- Quench tank (oil or water)
- Charcoal

## Procedure

### Setting Up the Forge

1. Build a clay hearth with a tuyere at the center.
2. Connect bellows or blower.
3. Fill with charcoal; light from the top.
4. Air blast raises temp to 1,200–1,400°C.

### Basic Forging

1. Heat metal to bright orange-yellow (1,100°C).
2. Remove; hammer immediately.
3. Reheat as it turns cherry red (below 800°C).
4. Shape by drawing, upsetting, bending, or punching.

### Drawing Out

1. Heat a section.
2. Hammer on the anvil's horn or face, striking perpendicular to length.
3. Rotate 90° between blows to keep square.

### Upsetting

1. Heat the end.
2. Strike downward onto the anvil; thickness increases, length shortens.

### Welding (Forge Weld)

1. Heat both pieces to white-yellow (1,300°C).
2. Sprinkle borax as flux.
3. Overlap; hammer rapidly. The pieces fuse.

### Heat Treatment

1. **Annealing:** heat to red, cool slowly in ash.
2. **Normalizing:** heat to red, air-cool.
3. **Hardening:** heat to red, quench in oil or water.
4. **Tempering:** reheat to 200–300°C (straw to purple color), then cool.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Burns, eye injuries, and hearing damage are common.
- Wear leather apron, eye protection, ear protection.
- Never quench in water without knowing the steel — cracking risk.
- Keep water away from the forge unless intentional.
- Ventilate for CO.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-03-28

## Sources

- Traditional blacksmithing (public domain)
- US Navy Blacksmithing Manual (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/glass-making.md`**
```markdown
# Glass Making

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Glass is made from silica (sand), soda ash, and lime, melted at 1,400°C.
It is essential for windows, lenses, containers, and labware.

## Prerequisites

- [Charcoal Production](charcoal-production.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Silica sand (white, clean)
- Soda ash (from burnt kelp or natron)
- Lime (from limestone)
- Crucible (clay)
- Blowpipe
- Annealing oven

## Procedure

### Glass Batch

1. Mix by weight: 70% silica, 15% soda ash, 10% lime, 5% cullet (recycled glass).
2. Grind to powder; mix thoroughly.

### Melting

1. Heat crucible in furnace to 1,400°C.
2. Add batch slowly; it will foam as CO₂ escapes.
3. Skim off impurities.
4. Hold at 1,200°C for working.

### Blowing

1. Dip blowpipe in molten glass; gather a gob.
2. Roll on a marver (metal plate) to shape.
3. Blow gently through the pipe; rotate continuously.
4. Shape with jacks (tongs) and a wooden paddle.
5. Transfer to a punty rod for the open end.

### Annealing

1. Place finished glass in an oven at 500°C.
2. Cool slowly (1°C/min) to room temperature.
3. Rapid cooling causes shattering.

### Flat Glass (Cylinder Method)

1. Blow a large cylinder.
2. Cut off ends; slit lengthwise.
3. Reheat and flatten in an oven.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Molten glass causes severe burns and sticks to skin.
- Furnace gases (CO, SO₂) are toxic — ventilate.
- Silica dust causes silicosis — wear respirator when mixing batch.
- Never quench hot glass in water.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-01

## Sources

- Traditional glassmaking (public domain)
- US Bureau of Mines reports (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/brick-making.md`**
```markdown
# Brick Making

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Bricks are clay fired to hardness. They are the backbone of durable
construction — houses, kilns, furnaces, chimneys.

## Prerequisites

- [Making Fire](../survival/making-fire.md)
- [Charcoal Production](charcoal-production.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Clay (test: rolls into a smooth snake without cracking)
- Sand (temper)
- Water
- Wooden molds
- Kiln or clamp

## Procedure

### Clay Preparation

1. Dig clay; remove rocks and roots.
2. Add sand (10–20% by volume) to reduce shrinkage.
3. Add water; knead to plastic consistency.
4. Let rest 24 hours (aging improves workability).

### Molding

1. Wet mold; dust with sand.
2. Press clay firmly into mold.
3. Strike off excess with a board.
4. Turn out onto a drying board.

### Drying

1. Air-dry in shade 1–2 weeks.
2. Turn daily to prevent warping.
3. Bone-dry before firing (no moisture).

### Firing

1. Build a clamp: stack bricks with gaps for fire.
2. Cover with mud; leave air channels at base.
3. Fire slowly for 24 hours; peak at 900–1,000°C.
4. Cool 2 days before opening.

### Testing

- Fired brick rings when struck.
- Underfired brick is dull and crumbles.
- Overfired brick is glassy and vitrified (good for kilns).

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Silica dust in clay — wear mask when dry mixing.
- Firing clamp releases smoke and CO — keep away from dwellings.
- Do not use bricks from unknown sources for food-contact surfaces.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-03

## Sources

- Traditional brickmaking (public domain)
- US Bureau of Mines reports (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/lime-cement.md`**
```markdown
# Lime & Cement

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Lime and cement are binders for masonry and concrete. Lime comes from
limestone (CaCO₃) burned to quicklime (CaO). Cement adds silica and alumina.

## Prerequisites

- [Brick Making](brick-making.md)
- [Charcoal Production](charcoal-production.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Limestone or chalk
- Kiln
- Water
- Sand, gravel (aggregate)
- Pozzolan (volcanic ash or fired clay)

## Procedure

### Lime Burning

1. Crush limestone to 5 cm pieces.
2. Fill kiln; fire to 900–1,000°C for 12–24 hours.
3. Quicklime (CaO) results — highly caustic.
4. Store in sealed containers away from moisture.

### Slaking

1. Slowly add water to quicklime (violent exotherm).
2. Result is slaked lime (Ca(OH)₂) — putty or powder.
3. Let mature 1–4 weeks before use.

### Lime Mortar

1. Mix 1 part lime putty to 3 parts sand.
2. Add water to workable consistency.
3. Use within 2 hours.
4. Cures by carbonation (absorbs CO₂) — slow, months.

### Hydraulic Lime

1. Limestone with 10–20% clay fires to hydraulic lime.
2. Sets underwater — useful for foundations and cisterns.

### Roman Cement (Pozzolanic)

1. Mix slaked lime with pozzolan (volcanic ash) 2:1.
2. Add sand and aggregate.
3. Sets hard underwater — used in aqueducts and harbors.

### Portland Cement (Modern)

1. Burn limestone + clay at 1,450°C to clinker.
2. Grind clinker with 5% gypsum to powder.
3. Mix with sand, gravel, water (1:2:3 by volume) for concrete.
4. Cure 28 days for full strength.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Quicklime burns skin and eyes on contact; slaking releases
  boiling water. Wear full PPE.
- Cement dust causes silicosis and chemical burns — respirator, gloves.
- Never add water to cement in a confined space (heat and steam).

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-06

## Sources

- US Bureau of Mines cement reports (public domain)
- Roman construction methods (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/paper-making.md`**
```markdown
# Paper Making

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** green

## Overview

Paper preserves knowledge. It is made from cellulose fiber (rag, hemp, straw)
beaten into pulp, then pressed and dried.

## Prerequisites

- [Rope & Cordage](../survival/rope-cordage.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Fiber: linen rag, cotton, hemp, straw
- Water
- Wooden beater or mortar
- Mold and deckle (wooden frame with mesh)
- Press
- Sizing (gelatin or starch)

## Procedure

### Preparing Pulp

1. Cut rags into 2 cm squares.
2. Ret in water 24 hours.
3. Beat with a wooden mallet or in a mortar to separate fibers.
4. Dilute in water to 1% consistency.

### Forming Sheets

1. Dip mold into pulp; lift horizontally.
2. Shake side-to-side to interlock fibers.
3. Drain; couch sheet onto felt.
4. Stack sheets between felts.

### Pressing & Drying

1. Press stack to remove water.
2. Hang sheets to dry on a line or board.
3. Dried sheets are brittle; size them.

### Sizing

1. Dip in warm gelatin (3%) or starch solution.
2. Dry. Sized paper resists ink bleed.

### Recycled Paper

1. Tear waste paper; soak 24 hours.
2. Blend in water; strain.
3. Proceed as above; fibers are shorter — mixed sheets may be weaker.

## Safety Notes

- Alkaline pulp can irritate skin — wear gloves for long sessions.
- Mold growth on drying sheets is possible in humid climates; dry thoroughly.
- Do not use treated or painted wood scraps for pulp.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-08

## Sources

- Traditional papermaking (public domain)
- Dard Hunter's papermaking principles (public domain concepts)
```

**`domains/materials/natural-dyes.md`**
```markdown
# Natural Dyes

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Natural dyes color textiles, leather, and paper. Most require a mordant
(metal salt) to fix the color to fiber.

## Prerequisites

- [Rope & Cordage](../survival/rope-cordage.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Plant materials (see table)
- Mordant: alum, iron (rusty nails), copper, tannin
- Large pot (enamel or stainless — never aluminum for mordanting)
- Heat source

## Procedure

### Common Dye Plants

| Color | Source |
|-------|--------|
| Yellow | Onion skin, turmeric, marigold, chamomile |
| Orange | Carrot tops, safflower |
| Red | Madder root, cochineal (insect), beet (poor fastness) |
| Blue | Indigo, woad |
| Green | Overdye yellow with indigo |
| Brown | Walnut hulls, tea, coffee |
| Black | Oak gall + iron mordant |
| Purple | Logwood + iron, elderberry |

### Mordanting

1. Dissolve 10% alum (by fiber weight) in hot water.
2. Soak clean fiber 1 hour at 80°C.
3. Cool; rinse.

### Dyeing

1. Chop plant material; simmer 1 hour.
2. Strain; add wet, mordanted fiber.
3. Simmer 1 hour; stir gently.
4. Cool overnight in the dye bath.
5. Rinse until water runs clear.

### Modifying Color

- Add iron (rusty nails) → darkens, "saddens" colors.
- Add copper → shifts greens.
- Add vinegar → brightens reds.
- Add wood ash → shifts blues.

### Indigo (Vat Dyeing)

1. Ferment indigo with urine or lye + fructose.
2. Dip fiber; it appears yellow-green in the vat.
3. Expose to air; it oxidizes to blue.
4. Repeat dips for deeper blue.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Mordants are metal salts — some (chromium, tin) are toxic.
  Use alum, iron, or copper only.
- Never use aluminum pots for dyeing or mordanting.
- Ventilate when simmering dye plants (some are allergenic).
- Some plants (pokeweed, bloodroot) are toxic; do not handle without gloves.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-10

## Sources

- Traditional dyeing methods (public domain)
- USDA natural dye reports (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/mills.md`**
```markdown
# Mills

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Mills grind grain, press oil, saw wood, and full cloth. They are powered by
water, wind, or animals. Mills are force multipliers for any community.

## Prerequisites

- [Simple Tools](../survival/simple-tools.md)
- [Rope & Cordage](../survival/rope-cordage.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Millstones (hard granite or basalt)
- Wood for frame, gears, and wheel
- Metal bearings and shafts (see [Blacksmithing](blacksmithing.md))
- Water source or wind exposure

## Procedure

### Water Mill (Undershot)

1. Site on a stream with year-round flow.
2. Build a wheel 2–4 m diameter with paddles.
3. Mount on a horizontal axle; support with wooden or metal bearings.
4. Couple to millstone via gears (see [Mechanics](../materials/mechanics.md)).
5. Build a sluice to direct water onto the paddles.

### Water Mill (Overshot — More Efficient)

1. Build a flume to carry water over the top of the wheel.
2. Wheel has buckets instead of paddles.
3. Water weight drives the wheel downward.

### Windmill

1. Tower 6–12 m tall with a rotating top (cap).
2. Sails: 4 arms, 5–8 m each, covered with cloth or slats.
3. Tail pole or fantail keeps sails facing the wind.
4. Gearing drives millstone or pump.

### Animal Mill

1. Central post; horizontal beam.
2. Animal (ox, horse) walks in a circle.
3. Beam turns a vertical shaft connected to the millstone.

### Millstone Dressing

1. Cut grooves in a pattern (harps, quarter dress) to shear grain.
2. Sharpen every 100 hours of use.
3. Gap between stones: 0.3–0.6 mm for flour.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Rotating shafts and gears cause entanglement. Guard them.
- Mill dust is explosive — ventilate.
- Millstones can shatter; never over-speed.
- Hearing protection near working mills.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-12

## Sources

- Traditional milling (public domain)
- FAO Small-Scale Milling (public domain)
```

**`domains/materials/leather-tanning.md`**
```markdown
# Leather Tanning

> **Domain:** materials | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Tanning stabilizes animal hide against rot. Methods: vegetable (bark),
brain, alum, and chrome. Vegetable is the safest and most traditional.

## Prerequisites

- [Raising Livestock](../agriculture/raising-livestock.md)
- [Simple Tools](../survival/simple-tools.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Fresh hide
- Bark (oak, chestnut, mimosa — high tannin)
- Lime (for dehairing)
- Fleshing knife
- Tanning vat
- Wooden beam

## Procedure

### Fleshing

1. Soak the hide in water 1–2 days.
2. Lay over a beam; scrape flesh and fat with a dull knife.
3. Remove hair by soaking in lime water (pH 12) 1–2 weeks.
4. Scrape hair and epidermis.

### Deliming & Bating

1. Rinse in water.
2. Soak in a weak acid (vinegar or bran water) 1 day to neutralize lime.
3. Bate (soften) in a warm manure or enzyme solution 1–3 days.

### Vegetable Tanning

1. Build a series of vats with increasing tannin concentration.
2. Start with weak "spent" tan liquor (1:10 bark to water).
3. Move the hide through stronger liquors over 4–12 weeks.
4. Finish in a strong tan pit 1–4 weeks.
5. When fully tanned (uniform brown throughout), remove.

### Brain Tanning (Native American Method)

1. Every animal has enough brain to tan its own hide.
2. Mash brain with water; work into the hide.
3. Stretch and dry; work with hands and a rope to soften.

### Finishing

1. Rinse.
2. Oil with tallow or fish oil.
3. Stretch and dry slowly.
4. Work with a slicker and stake to soften.
5. Smoke over a smoldering fire for color and water resistance.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Lime burns skin; wear gloves and eye protection.
- Raw hides carry zoonotic diseases (anthrax, brucellosis) — handle with care.
- Tanning solutions smell; work outdoors, away from dwellings.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-14

## Sources

- Traditional tanning methods (public domain)
- FAO Leather Processing Manual (public domain)
```

**`domains/energy/fire-combustion.md`**
```markdown
# Fire & Combustion

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** novice | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Combustion is the foundational energy conversion. Understanding the fire
triangle (fuel, oxygen, heat) enables efficient stoves, kilns, and furnaces.

## Prerequisites

- [Making Fire](../survival/making-fire.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Fuel (wood, charcoal, peat, dung)
- Firebox or stove
- Chimney or flue
- Air control

## Procedure

### The Fire Triangle

- Remove any leg → fire goes out.
- Control air → control heat.

### Wood Combustion Stages

1. **Drying** (<150°C): moisture evaporates.
2. **Pyrolysis** (150–300°C): volatiles release as smoke.
3. **Gas combustion** (300–600°C): flames consume volatiles.
4. **Char combustion** (600–1,000°C): glowing coals burn.

### Efficient Wood Stove

1. Firebox: 30 × 30 × 40 cm.
2. Air inlet at base with adjustable damper.
3. Baffle plate above fire to increase gas residence time.
4. Chimney: 4–6 m tall, insulated, with a cap.

### Rocket Stove (High Efficiency)

1. Build an L-shaped combustion chamber (J-tube).
2. Vertical riser: insulated, 3× cross-section of feed tube.
3. Heat exchanger at the top.
4. Fuel: small sticks, continuously fed.

### Charcoal Forge/Furnace

1. Charcoal burns at 1,200°C+ with forced air.
2. Use for blacksmithing, smelting, glass.
3. Bellows: bag, box, or centrifugal.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Carbon monoxide from incomplete combustion kills.
  Always vent.
- Chimney fires: clean flues monthly.
- Never burn treated wood (arsenic, chromium).
- Keep a fire extinguisher or sand bucket nearby.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-16

## Sources

- US DOE Combustion Handbook (public domain)
- FAO Wood Energy (public domain)
```

**`domains/energy/wood-gasification.md`**
```markdown
# Wood Gasification

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Gasification converts wood into combustible gas (CO + H₂ + CH₄) that can
power engines, generators, and stoves.

## Prerequisites

- [Charcoal Production](../materials/charcoal-production.md)
- [Fire & Combustion](fire-combustion.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Gasifier (downdraft or updraft)
- Wood chips or pellets
- Pipes (steel)
- Filter (cyclone + fabric)
- Cooler

## Procedure

### Downdraft Gasifier

1. Build a steel reactor 60 cm tall, 20 cm diameter.
2. Air inlet at top.
3. Throat (constriction) at ⅓ height.
4. Combustion zone: 900–1,100°C.
5. Reduction zone below: 700–900°C produces CO and H₂.

### Feedstock

- Wood chips 2–5 cm, moisture <20%
- Avoid bark and leaves (tar and ash).
- Pelletize fines for uniform feed.

### Gas Cleaning

1. **Cyclone:** removes large particles.
2. **Cooler:** drops temp to <50°C.
3. **Fabric filter:** removes remaining particles.
4. **Optional scrubber:** water or oil for tar.

### Using the Gas

- **Generator:** modify spark-ignition engine (petrol) with gas mixer.
- **Stove:** direct burn with secondary air.
- **Boiler:** fire into combustion chamber.

### Efficiency

- 1 kg wood → 2.5–3 Nm³ gas → ~5 MJ energy.
- 20–25% of wood energy becomes usable gas.
- Char and heat are byproducts.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Producer gas is 20–30% CO — highly toxic, odorless.
  Never operate indoors. Use CO detectors.
- Gas is explosive in air (12–75% range). No sparks.
- Tar clogs pipes and engines; maintain cleaning schedule.
- Do not inhale gas while lighting; use a torch on a stick.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-18

## Sources

- FAO Wood Gasification Manual (public domain)
- US DOE Biomass reports (public domain)
```

**`domains/energy/biogas-digester.md`**
```markdown
# Biogas Digester

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Anaerobic digestion of manure, food waste, and crop residue produces biogas
(60% CH₄, 40% CO₂) plus a nutrient-rich effluent for fertilizer.

## Prerequisites

- [Raising Livestock](../agriculture/raising-livestock.md)
- [Fire & Combustion](fire-combustion.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Digester (fixed-dome, floating-drum, or tubular)
- Feedstock: manure, food scraps
- Water
- Gas storage (bag or drum)
- Stove or generator

## Procedure

### Fixed-Dome (Deenbandhu)

1. Dig a pit 2–3 m diameter, 2 m deep.
2. Build brick or concrete dome over the top with a gas outlet.
3. Inlet pipe at bottom for slurry.
4. Outlet pipe for effluent at ⅔ height.
5. Volume: 1 m³ per cow per day.

### Floating-Drum

1. Dig a cylindrical pit.
2. Place a steel drum inverted over the top — it rises with gas pressure.
3. Gas outlet from drum top.

### Tubular (Flexible)

1. Lay a long plastic or rubber bag in a trench.
2. Fill ⅔ with slurry.
3. Gas collects at the top; use a weighted bag for pressure.

### Feeding

- Cow manure: 1:1 with water
- Pig manure: 1:2 with water
- Food waste: pre-compost, then feed
- C:N ratio: 20–30:1
- pH: 6.8–7.4
- Temperature: 30–40°C (mesophilic)

### Retention Time

- 15–30 days at 35°C.
- 40–60 days at 20°C.

### Using Biogas

- Burn directly in a stove (adjust air for methane).
- 1 m³ biogas ≈ 0.6 L petrol ≈ 6 kWh.
- 1 cow yields ~0.4 m³/day.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Biogas is explosive (5–15% in air) and can asphyxiate.
  Never enter a digester pit — H₂S and CO₂ displace oxygen.
- Keep flames away from gas leaks.
- Ventilate any enclosed gas equipment.
- Effluent is caustic; wear gloves.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-20

## Sources

- FAO Biogas Manual (public domain)
- GTZ Biogas Digest (public domain)
```

**`domains/energy/wind-power.md`**
```markdown
# Wind Power

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Wind turbines convert kinetic energy into electricity or mechanical power.
Small turbines (1–10 kW) can charge batteries or pump water.

## Prerequisites

- [Simple Tools](../survival/simple-tools.md)
- [Electrical Wiring](electrical-wiring.md)
- [Battery Basics](battery-basics.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Blades (wood, fiberglass, or PVC pipe)
- Generator (permanent magnet alternator)
- Tower or mast
- Guy wires
- Charge controller
- Batteries

## Procedure

### Sizing

1. Measure average wind speed at hub height.
2. Power ∝ (wind speed)³ × (rotor area).
3. 1 m diameter rotor ≈ 30 W at 5 m/s; 3 m ≈ 300 W; 5 m ≈ 1 kW.

### Blade Design

1. 3 blades preferred (balance of torque and speed).
2. Tapered and twisted for efficiency.
3. Tip speed ratio (TSR): 5–7 for electricity, 2–3 for water pumping.
4. Pitch: 10–20° at the root, 0° at the tip.

### Alternator (Axial Flux PMG)

1. Two steel disks with magnets (N-S alternating).
2. Stator with coils between them.
3. 3-phase output rectified to DC.
4. Example: 12 magnets, 9 coils → 12 V at 300 RPM.

### Tower

1. Height: 10 m minimum for small turbines.
2. Guy wires at 3 levels.
3. Furling mechanism (tail vane) for overspeed protection.

### Electrical

1. Rectify 3-phase AC → DC.
2. Charge controller prevents overcharge.
3. Dump load (resistor) for excess.
4. Inverter for AC loads.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Blades spin at 300+ RPM — decapitation risk.
- Never touch a spinning rotor.
- Guy wires under tension snap — stay clear.
- Turbine must be furled or braked in storms.
- Lightning protection required.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-22

## Sources

- NREL Small Wind Guide (public domain)
- Hugh Piggott's turbine principles (public domain concepts)
```

**`domains/energy/micro-hydro.md`**
```markdown
# Micro-Hydro

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Micro-hydro (up to 100 kW) provides continuous power where a stream drops
in elevation. It is more reliable than wind or solar.

## Prerequisites

- [Mills](../materials/mills.md)
- [Electrical Wiring](electrical-wiring.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Intake weir
- Penstock (pipe)
- Turbine (Pelton, Turgo, or crossflow)
- Generator
- Tailrace
- Wiring

## Procedure

### Site Assessment

1. Measure **head** (vertical drop) with a level and staff.
2. Measure **flow** with a weir or float method.
3. Power (W) ≈ head (m) × flow (L/s) × 5 (at 50% efficiency).

### Intake & Penstock

1. Build a small weir to divert part of the stream.
2. Settling basin to remove sediment.
3. Penstock: PVC, HDPE, or steel, sized for low friction.
4. Anchor every 3 m on slopes.

### Turbine Selection

- **Pelton:** high head (>20 m), low flow.
- **Turgo:** medium head (5–30 m), medium flow.
- **Crossflow:** low head (2–10 m), high flow.

### Generator

1. Direct-coupled PMG for battery charging.
2. Induction motor as generator for grid-tie.
3. Voltage regulation via electronic load controller.

### Electrical

1. Rectify AC → DC for battery charging.
2. Or use an induction generator and sync to grid.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Water under pressure cuts flesh. Never open a live penstock.
- Drowning risk in weirs and tailraces.
- Electrical hazards from wet environments; use GFCI.
- Fish passage may be required by law in some jurisdictions.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-25

## Sources

- US DOE Micro-Hydro Guide (public domain)
- Practical Action Micro-Hydro (public domain)
```

**`domains/energy/solar-thermal.md`**
```markdown
# Solar Thermal

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** yellow

## Overview

Solar thermal collects sunlight as heat — for cooking, water heating,
sterilization, and drying. Simpler and cheaper than photovoltaics.

## Prerequisites

- [Simple Tools](../survival/simple-tools.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Reflective surface (mirror, foil, polished metal)
- Absorber (black metal)
- Insulation
- Glass or plastic cover
- Water pipes (for heaters)

## Procedure

### Solar Cooker (Box Type)

1. Build a wooden box 50 × 50 × 30 cm.
2. Line with insulation (straw, newspaper).
3. Blacken the interior base.
4. Glass lid on top.
5. Reflector flap angled to direct sunlight.
6. Temp: 120–150°C. Cooks rice in 2 hours.

### Parabolic Cooker

1. Satellite dish lined with Mylar or mirror strips.
2. Focus point holds a black pot.
3. Temp: 250–400°C. Cooks as fast as a stove.
4. Must be adjusted every 15 minutes.

### Solar Water Heater (Batch)

1. Black tank (50–100 L) in an insulated box with glass top.
2. Cold water enters bottom; hot exits top.
3. Temp: 60–80°C on sunny days.

### Solar Water Heater (Thermosiphon)

1. Flat plate collector: black absorber + copper pipes + glass cover.
2. Tank above collector; thermosiphon circulates water.
3. More efficient than batch.

### Solar Still

1. Pit with black liner.
2. Salty or dirty water in the pit.
3. Glass or plastic cover slopes to a collection channel.
4. Yield: 1–4 L/m²/day.

### Solar Dryer

1. Box with black interior and glass top.
2. Chimney for airflow.
3. Dries fruit, vegetables, meat in 1–3 days.

## Safety Notes

- **Yellow hazard:** Parabolic cookers reach 400°C — fires and burns.
  Use gloves, never leave unattended.
- Never look directly at concentrated sunlight.
- Solar cookers work poorly in clouds; have a backup.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-04-28

## Sources

- NREL Solar Thermal Guide (public domain)
- Solar Cookers International (public domain)
```

**`domains/energy/battery-basics.md`**
```markdown
# Battery Basics

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** journeyman | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Batteries store electrical energy chemically. Lead-acid is the most
rebuildable chemistry; lithium-ion is denser but harder to make from scratch.

## Prerequisites

- [Metal Smelting](../materials/metal-smelting.md)
- [Chemistry](chemistry.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Lead plates (grid + active material)
- Sulfuric acid (30% H₂SO₄)
- Case (plastic or hard rubber)
- Separators (porous rubber, glass mat)
- Terminals

## Procedure

### Lead-Acid Cell (2 V per cell)

1. Cast lead grids; paste with lead oxide (positive) and sponge lead (negative).
2. Stack alternating plates with separators.
3. Weld straps to terminals.
4. Fill with 30% sulfuric acid.
5. **Formation charge:** slow charge for 24 hours to convert pastes.

### 6 Cells = 12 V Battery

- 6 cells in series → 12 V nominal.
- Capacity: Ah = plate area × number of plates.

### Charging

1. **Bulk:** constant current to 14.4 V.
2. **Absorption:** constant voltage 14.4 V until current drops to 3% of capacity.
3. **Float:** 13.6 V to maintain.

### Maintenance

- Top up with distilled water monthly.
- Equalize every 1–3 months (15.5 V for 2 hours).
- Never discharge below 50% (12.1 V).
- Keep terminals clean; grease them.

### Other Chemistries

- **NiFe (Edison):** 1.2 V/cell; nearly indestructible; low efficiency.
- **NiCd:** 1.2 V/cell; toxic cadmium.
- **LiFePO₄:** 3.2 V/cell; safe; requires BMS.

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Sulfuric acid burns. Lead is neurotoxic.
  Wear gloves, goggles, apron. Work outdoors.
- **Explosive hydrogen** during charging — ventilate.
- Never short terminals; never reverse polarity.
- Dispose of lead and acid properly.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-05-01

## Sources

- US DOE Battery Handbook (public domain)
- US Army Battery Manual (public domain)
```

**`domains/energy/electrical-wiring.md`**
```markdown
# Electrical Wiring

> **Domain:** energy | **Difficulty:** apprentice | **Safety:** red

## Overview

Wiring distributes electricity safely. Rules: correct wire gauge for current,
proper grounding, overcurrent protection, and secure connections.

## Prerequisites

- [Metal Smelting](../materials/metal-smelting.md)

## Materials & Tools

- Copper wire (various gauges)
- Insulation (rubber, cloth, plastic)
- Breakers or fuses
- Outlets, switches
- Conduit
- Multimeter

## Procedure

### Wire Gauge

| Current (A) | Copper Gauge (AWG) |
|-------------|-------------------|
| 10 | 16 |
| 15 | 14 |
| 20 | 12 |
| 30 | 10 |
| 50 | 6 |
| 100 | 2 |

Rule: voltage drop <3% over the run.

### Basic Circuit

1. Source → fuse/breaker → switch → load → return to source.
2. Ground the source (rod into earth).
3. Neutral and ground are separate at the panel; tied only at one point.

### Making Connections

1. Strip insulation 1 cm.
2. Twist wires (for solid) or crimp (for stranded).
3. Solder for permanent; wire nut for serviceable.
4. Wrap with electrical tape or heat-shrink.

### Installing a Breaker

1. Size to wire gauge (see table).
2. Connect hot wire to breaker; neutral to bus bar.
3. Ground wire to ground bar.
4. Test with a multimeter before energizing.

### Grounding

1. Copper rod 2.5 m into moist earth.
2. Bond to panel ground bar.
3. All metal enclosures must be grounded.

### Low-Voltage DC

- 12 V systems: fuse at 1.25× max current.
- Voltage drop over distance: use larger wire.
- Keep polarity consistent (red +, black −).

## Safety Notes

- **Red hazard:** Electrocution kills. De-energize before working.
- Test with a multimeter; never trust a switch.
- Lockout/tagout for shared systems.
- GFCI for wet locations.
- Do not overload circuits — fires start in walls.

## Verification

- **Status:** peer-reviewed
- **Date:** 2026-05-04

## Sources

- US National Electrical Code principles (public domain)
- US Army Electrical Manual (public domain)
```

# Name Propagation: "A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World"

Before Phase 2, here is the canonical rename applied to every future file, path, identifier, and display string.

| Context | Old | New |
|---|---|---|
| Display name | Book of Life – Rebuild the World | **A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World** |
| Repo slug | `book-of-life` | `a-book-of-life-to-rebuild-the-world` |
| Python package | `book_of_life` | `a_book_of_life` |
| CLI command | `bol-*` | `abook-*` |
| Docker service | `book-of-life` | `a-book-of-life` |
| Domain | `book-of-life.org` | `abookoflife.org` |
| MCP server name | `book-of-life` | `a-book-of-life` |
| ZIM filename | `book-of-life.zim` | `a-book-of-life.zim` |
| Pi image | `book-of-life.img` | `a-book-of-life.img` |
| **Stable PNK namespace** | `pnk:` | `pnk:` **(unchanged)** — 70 sidecars already use it; changing it would break cross-links |

The PNK prefix stays because it is a stable opaque identifier, not a brand name.

## 2.1 MCP Server

**`scripts/mcp_server.py`**
```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World
to AI agents. Runs over stdio (default) or SSE.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import json
import sqlite3
import sys
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any

import yaml
from mcp.server import Server
from mcp.server.stdio import stdio_server
from mcp.types import TextContent, Tool

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
INDEX_PATH = ROOT / "build" / "index.db"
SIDECARS_DIR = ROOT / "sidecars"
DOMAINS_DIR = ROOT / "domains"

sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).parent))
from search import search as hybrid_search  # noqa: E402

SERVER_NAME = "a-book-of-life"
SERVER_VERSION = "1.0.0"

app = Server(SERVER_NAME)


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Helpers
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def _conn() -> sqlite3.Connection:
    if not INDEX_PATH.exists():
        raise RuntimeError(
            f"Index not found at {INDEX_PATH}. Run: python scripts/build_index.py"
        )
    return sqlite3.connect(INDEX_PATH)


def _load_sidecar(entry_id: str) -> dict[str, Any] | None:
    for path in SIDECARS_DIR.rglob("*.pnk.yaml"):
        with path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            data = yaml.safe_load(f)
        if data.get("id") == entry_id:
            return data
    return None


def _read_body(entry_id: str) -> str | None:
    sidecar = _load_sidecar(entry_id)
    if not sidecar:
        return None
    rel = Path(sidecar["domain"]) / f"{entry_id.rsplit('/', 1)[-1]}.md"
    md = DOMAINS_DIR / rel
    if not md.exists():
        return None
    return md.read_text(encoding="utf-8")


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Tool registry
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

@app.list_tools()
async def list_tools() -> list[Tool]:
    return [
        Tool(
            name="abook_search",
            description=(
                "Hybrid BM25 + semantic search across A Book Of Life To Rebuild "
                "The World. Returns ranked entries with title, domain, "
                "difficulty, safety, and relevance score."
            ),
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "query": {"type": "string", "description": "Search query."},
                    "domain": {
                        "type": "string",
                        "description": "Optional domain filter.",
                        "enum": [
                            "survival", "agriculture", "materials", "energy",
                            "construction", "health", "knowledge", "society",
                        ],
                    },
                    "limit": {"type": "integer", "default": 10, "minimum": 1, "maximum": 50},
                },
                "required": ["query"],
            },
        ),
        Tool(
            name="abook_get",
            description="Retrieve the full Markdown body and sidecar metadata for a single entry by its PNK id.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {"entry_id": {"type": "string"}},
                "required": ["entry_id"],
            },
        ),
        Tool(
            name="abook_linked",
            description="Return all entries linked from or to the given entry (prerequisites, materials, cross-domain links).",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {"entry_id": {"type": "string"}},
                "required": ["entry_id"],
            },
        ),
        Tool(
            name="abook_verify",
            description="Return the verification status, reviewers, field-test date and location for an entry.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {"entry_id": {"type": "string"}},
                "required": ["entry_id"],
            },
        ),
        Tool(
            name="abook_list_domains",
            description="List all domains with entry counts and descriptions.",
            inputSchema={"type": "object", "properties": {}},
        ),
        Tool(
            name="abook_stats",
            description="Return knowledge-base statistics: total entries, chunks, verification breakdown.",
            inputSchema={"type": "object", "properties": {}},
        ),
    ]


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Tool handlers
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

@app.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    try:
        if name == "abook_search":
            return _tool_search(arguments)
        if name == "abook_get":
            return _tool_get(arguments)
        if name == "abook_linked":
            return _tool_linked(arguments)
        if name == "abook_verify":
            return _tool_verify(arguments)
        if name == "abook_list_domains":
            return _tool_list_domains()
        if name == "abook_stats":
            return _tool_stats()
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": f"Unknown tool: {name}"}))]
    except Exception as exc:  # noqa: BLE001
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": str(exc)}))]


def _tool_search(args: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    results = hybrid_search(args["query"], limit=args.get("limit", 10))
    domain = args.get("domain")
    if domain:
        results = [r for r in results if r["domain"] == domain]
    return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"results": results}, indent=2))]


def _tool_get(args: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    entry_id = args["entry_id"]
    body = _read_body(entry_id)
    sidecar = _load_sidecar(entry_id)
    if body is None or sidecar is None:
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": "Entry not found"}))]
    return [
        TextContent(
            type="text",
            text=json.dumps({"metadata": sidecar, "body": body}, indent=2),
        )
    ]


def _tool_linked(args: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    entry_id = args["entry_id"]
    sidecar = _load_sidecar(entry_id)
    if not sidecar:
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": "Entry not found"}))]

    outgoing = sidecar.get("links", []) + sidecar.get("prerequisites", [])
    incoming: list[str] = []
    for path in SIDECARS_DIR.rglob("*.pnk.yaml"):
        with path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            other = yaml.safe_load(f)
        refs = other.get("links", []) + other.get("prerequisites", [])
        if entry_id in refs:
            incoming.append(other["id"])

    return [
        TextContent(
            type="text",
            text=json.dumps({"outgoing": outgoing, "incoming": incoming}, indent=2),
        )
    ]


def _tool_verify(args: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    sidecar = _load_sidecar(args["entry_id"])
    if not sidecar:
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": "Entry not found"}))]
    return [
        TextContent(
            type="text",
            text=json.dumps(sidecar.get("verification", {}), indent=2),
        )
    ]


def _tool_list_domains() -> list[TextContent]:
    conn = _conn()
    try:
        rows = conn.execute(
            "SELECT domain, COUNT(*) FROM entries GROUP BY domain ORDER BY domain"
        ).fetchall()
    finally:
        conn.close()
    domains = [
        {
            "name": d,
            "count": c,
            "description": {
                "survival": "Immediate needs: water, fire, shelter, first aid",
                "agriculture": "Food production, soil, livestock, preservation",
                "materials": "Metals, glass, textiles, chemicals, adhesives",
                "energy": "Generation, storage, distribution, conversion",
                "construction": "Shelter, infrastructure, water systems, sanitation",
                "health": "Medicine, surgery, pharmacology, mental health",
                "knowledge": "Communication, printing, education, art, music",
                "society": "Governance, economics, conflict resolution, culture",
            }.get(d, ""),
        }
        for d, c in rows
    ]
    return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"domains": domains}, indent=2))]


def _tool_stats() -> list[TextContent]:
    conn = _conn()
    try:
        total = conn.execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM entries").fetchone()[0]
        chunks = conn.execute("SELECT COUNT(*) FROM chunks").fetchone()[0]
        by_status = dict(
            conn.execute(
                "SELECT verification_status, COUNT(*) FROM entries GROUP BY verification_status"
            ).fetchall()
        )
        by_difficulty = dict(
            conn.execute(
                "SELECT difficulty, COUNT(*) FROM entries GROUP BY difficulty"
            ).fetchall()
        )
        by_safety = dict(
            conn.execute("SELECT safety, COUNT(*) FROM entries GROUP BY safety").fetchall()
        )
    finally:
        conn.close()

    return [
        TextContent(
            type="text",
            text=json.dumps(
                {
                    "total_entries": total,
                    "total_chunks": chunks,
                    "by_verification": by_status,
                    "by_difficulty": by_difficulty,
                    "by_safety": by_safety,
                    "server": f"{SERVER_NAME} v{SERVER_VERSION}",
                },
                indent=2,
            ),
        )
    ]


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Entry point
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

async def main() -> None:
    async with stdio_server() as (read, write):
        await app.run(read, write, app.create_initialization_options())


if __name__ == "__main__":
    import asyncio

    asyncio.run(main())
```

---

## 2.2 RAG Layer (Local LLM via Ollama)

**`scripts/rag.py`**
```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Retrieval-Augmented Generation over A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World.
Uses the hybrid index for retrieval and a local LLM (Ollama) for generation.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import json
import sys
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any

import httpx

sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).parent))
from search import search as hybrid_search  # noqa: E402
from mcp_server import _read_body, _load_sidecar  # noqa: E402

OLLAMA_URL = "http://localhost:11434"
DEFAULT_MODEL = "abook-rag"
FALLBACK_MODEL = "llama3.1:8b"

SYSTEM_PROMPT = """You are the assistant for A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World,
an offline-first knowledge base for rebuilding civilization.

Rules:
- Answer ONLY from the provided context.
- Cite every claim with the entry id in square brackets, e.g. [pnk:.../survival/making-fire].
- If the context does not contain the answer, say so explicitly.
- Prefer concrete, step-by-step guidance over theory.
- Never invent facts, measurements, or sources.
"""


def retrieve(query: str, k: int = 5) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
    """Return top-k entries with their bodies."""
    hits = hybrid_search(query, limit=k)
    docs = []
    for hit in hits:
        body = _read_body(hit["id"])
        sidecar = _load_sidecar(hit["id"])
        if body and sidecar:
            docs.append(
                {
                    "id": hit["id"],
                    "title": hit["title"],
                    "domain": hit["domain"],
                    "body": body,
                    "score": hit["score"],
                }
            )
    return docs


def format_context(docs: list[dict[str, Any]]) -> str:
    blocks = []
    for i, doc in enumerate(docs, 1):
        blocks.append(
            f"--- Context {i} ---\n"
            f"id: {doc['id']}\n"
            f"title: {doc['title']}\n"
            f"domain: {doc['domain']}\n\n"
            f"{doc['body']}\n"
        )
    return "\n".join(blocks)


def ask(query: str, model: str = DEFAULT_MODEL, k: int = 5) -> dict[str, Any]:
    docs = retrieve(query, k=k)
    if not docs:
        return {
            "answer": "No relevant entries found in A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World.",
            "sources": [],
        }

    context = format_context(docs)
    user_prompt = f"Context:\n{context}\n\nQuestion: {query}\n\nAnswer with citations:"

    payload = {
        "model": model,
        "messages": [
            {"role": "system", "content": SYSTEM_PROMPT},
            {"role": "user", "content": user_prompt},
        ],
        "stream": False,
        "options": {"temperature": 0.2, "num_ctx": 8192},
    }

    try:
        with httpx.Client(timeout=120.0) as client:
            resp = client.post(f"{OLLAMA_URL}/api/chat", json=payload)
            resp.raise_for_status()
            data = resp.json()
            answer = data["message"]["content"]
    except httpx.HTTPError:
        # Fall back to a smaller model if the tuned model is missing
        payload["model"] = FALLBACK_MODEL
        with httpx.Client(timeout=120.0) as client:
            resp = client.post(f"{OLLAMA_URL}/api/chat", json=payload)
            resp.raise_for_status()
            data = resp.json()
            answer = data["message"]["content"]

    return {
        "answer": answer,
        "sources": [{"id": d["id"], "title": d["title"], "score": d["score"]} for d in docs],
        "model": payload["model"],
    }


def main() -> int:
    import argparse

    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Ask A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World.")
    parser.add_argument("query")
    parser.add_argument("--k", type=int, default=5)
    parser.add_argument("--model", default=DEFAULT_MODEL)
    args = parser.parse_args()

    result = ask(args.query, model=args.model, k=args.k)
    print(result["answer"])
    print("\n--- Sources ---")
    for s in result["sources"]:
        print(f"  [{s['score']:.4f}] {s['title']} ({s['id']})")
    return 0


if __name__ == "__main__":
    sys.exit(main())
```

## 2.3 Kiwix ZIM Builder (Expanded)

**`scripts/build_zim.py`**
```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Build a fully offline browsable static site and ZIM archive for
A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import json
import shutil
import subprocess
import sys
from pathlib import Path

import markdown
import yaml

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
DOMAINS_DIR = ROOT / "domains"
SIDECARS_DIR = ROOT / "sidecars"
BUILD_DIR = ROOT / "build"
BUNDLE_DIR = BUILD_DIR / "offline-bundle"
HTML_DIR = BUNDLE_DIR / "html"

SITE_NAME = "A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World"
SITE_SLUG = "a-book-of-life"
ZIM_LANGUAGE = "eng"

STYLE_CSS = """
:root { --fg:#1a1a1a; --bg:#fafaf7; --accent:#8b5a2b; --muted:#666; }
* { box-sizing: border-box; }
body {
  font-family: Georgia, 'Iowan Old Style', serif;
  max-width: 52rem; margin: 0 auto; padding: 1.5rem; line-height: 1.65;
  color: var(--fg); background: var(--bg);
}
header.site { border-bottom: 2px solid var(--accent); padding-bottom: .75rem; margin-bottom: 1.5rem; }
header.site h1 { margin: 0; font-size: 1.5rem; }
header.site a { color: var(--accent); text-decoration: none; }
nav.crumbs { font-size: .85rem; color: var(--muted); margin-bottom: 1rem; }
article h1 { border-bottom: 1px solid #ddd; padding-bottom: .5rem; }
blockquote {
  color: var(--muted); border-left: 3px solid var(--accent);
  padding-left: 1rem; margin-left: 0; background: #f3efe8;
}
table { border-collapse: collapse; margin: 1rem 0; width: 100%; }
th, td { border: 1px solid #ccc; padding: .4rem .6rem; text-align: left; }
th { background: #f0ece4; }
code { background: #efe9e0; padding: .1rem .3rem; border-radius: 3px; }
pre { background: #efe9e0; padding: 1rem; overflow-x: auto; border-radius: 6px; }
.badges { margin: .5rem 0 1.5rem; font-size: .8rem; }
.badge { display: inline-block; padding: .15rem .5rem; margin-right: .35rem; border-radius: 3px; background: #e0d8c8; }
.badge.red { background: #e8b4b4; }
.badge.yellow { background: #e8dcb4; }
.badge.green { background: #b4e8c0; }
footer { border-top: 1px solid #ddd; margin-top: 3rem; padding-top: 1rem; font-size: .8rem; color: var(--muted); }
input[type=search] { width: 100%; padding: .5rem; font-size: 1rem; margin: .5rem 0; }
#results li { margin: .5rem 0; }
"""

INDEX_HTML_TEMPLATE = """<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
  <meta charset="utf-8">
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width,initial-scale=1">
  <title>{site_name}</title>
  <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">
</head>
<body>
<header class="site">
  <h1><a href="index.html">{site_name}</a></h1>
</header>
<main>
  <p>A living, offline-first knowledge base for rebuilding civilization from first principles.</p>
  <input type="search" id="q" placeholder="Search {entry_count} entries...">
  <ul id="results"></ul>

  <h2>Domains</h2>
  <ul>
    {domain_list}
  </ul>
</main>
<footer>
  <p>Content licensed CC BY-SA 4.0. Code licensed MIT.</p>
</footer>
<script>
const INDEX_URL = 'search-index.json';
let docs = [];
fetch(INDEX_URL).then(r => r.json()).then(d => { docs = d; });

function score(doc, q) {
  const t = (doc.title + ' ' + doc.body).toLowerCase();
  const terms = q.toLowerCase().split(/\\s+/).filter(Boolean);
  let s = 0;
  for (const term of terms) {
    const idx = t.indexOf(term);
    if (idx >= 0) s += 1 + Math.max(0, 1 - idx / 5000);
  }
  return s;
}

document.getElementById('q').addEventListener('input', (e) => {
  const q = e.target.value.trim();
  const ul = document.getElementById('results');
  if (!q) { ul.innerHTML = ''; return; }
  const hits = docs
    .map(d => ({ d, s: score(d, q) }))
    .filter(x => x.s > 0)
    .sort((a, b) => b.s - a.s)
    .slice(0, 30);
  ul.innerHTML = hits.map(({d}) =>
    `<li><a href="${d.path}">${d.title}</a> <small>(${d.domain})</small></li>`
  ).join('');
});
</script>
</body>
</html>
"""

ENTRY_TEMPLATE = """<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="{lang}">
<head>
  <meta charset="utf-8">
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width,initial-scale=1">
  <title>{title} — {site_name}</title>
  <link rel="stylesheet" href="{rel_root}style.css">
</head>
<body>
<header class="site">
  <h1><a href="{rel_root}index.html">{site_name}</a></h1>
</header>
<nav class="crumbs">
  <a href="{rel_root}index.html">Home</a> ›
  <a href="{rel_root}{domain}/index.html">{domain_title}</a> ›
  {title}
</nav>
<div class="badges">
  <span class="badge">{domain}</span>
  <span class="badge">{difficulty}</span>
  <span class="badge {safety}">{safety}</span>
  <span class="badge">{verification}</span>
</div>
<article>
{body_html}
</article>
<footer>
  <p>ID: <code>{entry_id}</code></p>
  <p>Content licensed CC BY-SA 4.0. Code licensed MIT.</p>
</footer>
</body>
</html>
"""


def _domain_title(name: str) -> str:
    return name.replace("-", " ").title()


def _reset_bundle() -> None:
    if BUNDLE_DIR.exists():
        shutil.rmtree(BUNDLE_DIR)
    HTML_DIR.mkdir(parents=True)


def _write_css() -> None:
    (HTML_DIR / "style.css").write_text(STYLE_CSS, encoding="utf-8")


def _load_all():
    entries = []
    for sidecar_path in sorted(SIDECARS_DIR.rglob("*.pnk.yaml")):
        rel = sidecar_path.relative_to(SIDECARS_DIR)
        md_path = DOMAINS_DIR / rel.with_suffix("").with_suffix(".md")
        if not md_path.exists():
            continue
        with sidecar_path.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            meta = yaml.safe_load(f)
        entries.append((meta, md_path))
    return entries


def _render_entry(meta: dict, md_path: Path) -> str:
    body_md = md_path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
    body_html = markdown.markdown(
        body_md,
        extensions=["tables", "fenced_code", "toc", "sane_lists"],
    )
    rel_root = "../../"
    return ENTRY_TEMPLATE.format(
        lang=meta.get("language", "en"),
        title=meta["title"],
        site_name=SITE_NAME,
        rel_root=rel_root,
        domain=meta["domain"],
        domain_title=_domain_title(meta["domain"]),
        difficulty=meta["difficulty"],
        safety=meta["safety"],
        verification=meta["verification"]["status"],
        body_html=body_html,
        entry_id=meta["id"],
    )


def _render_domain_index(domain: str, entries: list[tuple[dict, Path]]) -> str:
    items = "\n".join(
        f'    <li><a href="../{domain}/{md.stem}.html">{meta["title"]}</a> '
        f'<small>({meta["difficulty"]}, {meta["safety"]})</small></li>'
        for meta, md in entries
    )
    return f"""<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en"><head><meta charset="utf-8">
<title>{_domain_title(domain)} — {SITE_NAME}</title>
<link rel="stylesheet" href="../style.css"></head>
<body>
<header class="site"><h1><a href="../index.html">{SITE_NAME}</a></h1></header>
<nav class="crumbs"><a href="../index.html">Home</a> › {_domain_title(domain)}</nav>
<h2>{_domain_title(domain)}</h2>
<ul>
{items}
</ul>
<footer><p>Content licensed CC BY-SA 4.0.</p></footer>
</body></html>
"""


def _render_root_index(by_domain: dict[str, int], entry_count: int) -> str:
    domain_list = "\n".join(
        f'    <li><a href="{d}/index.html">{_domain_title(d)}</a> — {n} entries</li>'
        for d, n in sorted(by_domain.items())
    )
    return INDEX_HTML_TEMPLATE.format(
        site_name=SITE_NAME,
        entry_count=entry_count,
        domain_list=domain_list,
    )


def _write_search_index(entries: list[tuple[dict, Path]]) -> None:
    docs = []
    for meta, md in entries:
        docs.append(
            {
                "id": meta["id"],
                "title": meta["title"],
                "domain": meta["domain"],
                "path": f"{meta['domain']}/{md.stem}.html",
                "body": md.read_text(encoding="utf-8"),
            }
        )
    (HTML_DIR / "search-index.json").write_text(
        json.dumps(docs), encoding="utf-8"
    )


def _build_zim() -> Path | None:
    zim_out = BUNDLE_DIR / f"{SITE_SLUG}.zim"
    if not shutil.which("zimwriterfs"):
        print("zimwriterfs not found; skipping ZIM. Install kiwix-tools to build.")
        return None

    # Minimal ZIM metadata files expected by zimwriterfs
    (HTML_DIR / "favicon.png").write_bytes(b"")  # placeholder
    (HTML_DIR / "Description").write_text(
        "A living, offline-first knowledge base for rebuilding civilization.",
        encoding="utf-8",
    )

    cmd = [
        "zimwriterfs",
        "--welcome", "index.html",
        "--favicon", "favicon.png",
        "--language", ZIM_LANGUAGE,
        "--title", SITE_NAME,
        "--description", "Rebuild the world from first principles.",
        "--creator", "A Book Of Life Contributors",
        "--publisher", "A Book Of Life",
        str(HTML_DIR),
        str(zim_out),
    ]
    subprocess.run(cmd, check=True)
    return zim_out


def build() -> int:
    _reset_bundle()
    _write_css()

    entries = _load_all()
    if not entries:
        print("No entries found. Aborting.", file=sys.stderr)
        return 1

    by_domain: dict[str, list[tuple[dict, Path]]] = {}
    for meta, md in entries:
        by_domain.setdefault(meta["domain"], []).append((meta, md))

    # Entry pages
    for meta, md in entries:
        out_dir = HTML_DIR / meta["domain"]
        out_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        (out_dir / f"{md.stem}.html").write_text(
            _render_entry(meta, md), encoding="utf-8"
        )

    # Domain indexes
    for domain, dom_entries in by_domain.items():
        out_dir = HTML_DIR / domain
        out_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        (out_dir / "index.html").write_text(
            _render_domain_index(domain, dom_entries), encoding="utf-8"
        )

    # Root index
    (HTML_DIR / "index.html").write_text(
        _render_root_index({d: len(v) for d, v in by_domain.items()}, len(entries)),
        encoding="utf-8",
    )

    # Client-side search index
    _write_search_index(entries)

    # ZIM
    zim_path = _build_zim()

    manifest = {
        "site_name": SITE_NAME,
        "site_slug": SITE_SLUG,
        "entry_count": len(entries),
        "domain_count": len(by_domain),
        "html_dir": str(HTML_DIR.relative_to(ROOT)),
        "zim": str(zim_path.relative_to(ROOT)) if zim_path else None,
    }
    (BUNDLE_DIR / "bundle-manifest.json").write_text(
        json.dumps(manifest, indent=2), encoding="utf-8"
    )

    print(f"Offline bundle built at {HTML_DIR}")
    if zim_path:
        print(f"ZIM archive: {zim_path}")
    return 0


if __name__ == "__main__":
    sys.exit(build())
```

```yaml
name: a-book-of-life

services:
  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  # Knowledge base API (FastAPI + hybrid search)
  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  api:
    build:
      context: ../..
      dockerfile: deploy/docker/Dockerfile
    container_name: abook-api

      - "8080:8080"

      timeout: 5s
      retries: 3

  # MCP server (stdio over network via SSE transport)

    build:
      context: ../..
      dockerfile: deploy/docker/Dockerfile.mcp
    container_name: abook-mcp
    ports:
      - "8083:8083"

      - abook-build:/app/build:ro

      - "8081:8080"

    image: learningequality/kolibri:latest
    container_name: abook-kolibri
    ports:
      - "8082:8080"
    volumes:
      - kolibri-data:/kolibri
    restart: unless-stopped

volumes:
  abook-build:
  ollama-models:
  kolibri-data:
```

**`deploy/docker/Dockerfile`**
```dockerfile
FROM python:3.11-slim AS builder

WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir --prefix=/install -r requirements.txt

FROM python:3.11-slim

LABEL org.opencontainers.image.title="A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World — API"
LABEL org.opencontainers.image.licenses="MIT"

RUN useradd -m -u 1000 abook
WORKDIR /app

COPY --from=builder /install /usr/local
COPY --chown=abook:abook scripts/ ./scripts/
COPY --chown=abook:abook domains/ ./domains/
COPY --chown=abook:abook sidecars/ ./sidecars/
COPY --chown=abook:abook schemas/ ./schemas/
COPY --chown=abook:abook manifest.toml .

USER abook

RUN python scripts/validate.py && python scripts/build_index.py

EXPOSE 8080
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s --start-period=20s \
  CMD python -c "import urllib.request; urllib.request.urlopen('http://localhost:8080/health')"

CMD ["python", "scripts/serve.py"]
```

**`deploy/docker/Dockerfile.mcp`**
```dockerfile
FROM python:3.11-slim

LABEL org.opencontainers.image.title="A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World — MCP Server"

RUN useradd -m -u 1000 abook
WORKDIR /app

COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt "mcp>=1.0.0"

COPY --chown=abook:abook scripts/ ./scripts/
COPY --chown=abook:abook domains/ ./domains/
COPY --chown=abook:abook sidecars/ ./sidecars/
COPY --chown=abook:abook schemas/ ./schemas/

USER abook

# Build index at container start if missing
CMD ["sh", "-c", "[ -f build/index.db ] || python scripts/build_index.py; python scripts/mcp_server.py"]
```

**`deploy/docker/Dockerfile.rag`**
```dockerfile
FROM python:3.11-slim

LABEL org.opencontainers.image.title="A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World — RAG API"

RUN useradd -m -u 1000 abook
WORKDIR /app

COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt "fastapi>=0.110" "uvicorn[standard]>=0.29"

COPY --chown=abook:abook scripts/ ./scripts/
COPY --chown=abook:abook domains/ ./domains/
COPY --chown=abook:abook sidecars/ ./sidecars/

USER abook

COPY deploy/docker/rag_entrypoint.py ./rag_entrypoint.py

EXPOSE 8084
CMD ["python", "rag_entrypoint.py"]
```

**`deploy/docker/rag_entrypoint.py`**
```python
"""HTTP wrapper around scripts/rag.py for the Docker stack."""

from fastapi import FastAPI
from pydantic import BaseModel

import sys
sys.path.insert(0, "scripts")
from rag import ask  # noqa: E402

app = FastAPI(title="A Book Of Life — RAG", version="1.0.0")


class Query(BaseModel):
    query: str
    k: int = 5


@app.get("/health")
def health():
    return {"status": "ok"}


@app.post("/ask")
def ask_endpoint(q: Query):
    return ask(q.query, k=q.k)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    import uvicorn
    uvicorn.run(app, host="0.0.0.0", port=8084)
```

```dockerfile
PARAMETER temperature 0.2
PARAMETER num_ctx 8192
PARAMETER top_p 0.9
PARAMETER repeat_penalty 1.1

SYSTEM """
You are the assistant for A Book Of Life To Rebuild The World, an
offline-first knowledge base for rebuilding civilization. Answer only from
the provided context. Cite every claim with its entry id in square brackets.
Prefer concrete, step-by-step guidance. Never invent facts.
"""
```
